# KCSC Proc — Project Specification
## Built on Frappe Framework / ERPNext v16

---

> **Document Status:** `v1.100`
> **Last Updated:** 2026-09-09
> **Maintained by:** KCSC — Karama Computer Services Company
> **App Name:** `proc_app`
> **Target Version:** ERPNext v16.30 / Frappe v16.29
> **Deployment Model:** Multi-client reusable app — standalone-capable, no dependency on any supplier portal. `supplier_portal` (VendorGate) optionally depends on this app; this app never depends on `supplier_portal`.
> **Update Policy:** This document MUST be updated whenever a feature, flow, architecture decision, or implementation detail changes. Every Claude Code session should begin by reading this file and end by proposing updates to it.

---

## 1. Project Overview

### 1.1 Purpose

KCSC Proc is the reusable back-office procurement system underlying KCSC's procurement solution suite. It covers Procurement Management (Purchase Requisition through Goods Receipt), Stock Management, and procurement/stock reporting, built entirely on ERPNext v16 out-of-the-box functionality wherever possible, with custom development limited to genuine gaps only.

KCSC Proc is designed to function as a complete, standalone procurement system via the ERPNext desk alone — a client may deploy it with or without a supplier-facing portal.

### 1.2 Relationship to supplier_portal (VendorGate)

`supplier_portal` is a separate, optional companion app that provides supplier self-service UI on top of KCSC Proc. The dependency is one-directional: `supplier_portal` depends on `proc_app`; `proc_app` never depends on `supplier_portal`. Full rationale and the ownership decision log for what belongs in which app is documented in `PROC_SUITE_ARCHITECTURE.md` (this app's repo), Section 1 (App Boundary & Ownership) — this content was originally written into `SUPPLIER_PORTAL_SPEC.md` Section 16 during Phase 2 of the supplier_portal project, then relocated here on 2026-08-06.

### 1.3 Status

This app was scaffolded on 2026-08-03 (re-scaffolded from the earlier `karama_proc` under a new name). It is now a substantially built, live system on `site1.local` — 6 custom doctypes (2 submittable, 2 child tables), 5 active Workflows covering Material Request/Purchase Order Amendment/Request for Quotation/RFQ Comparison Sheet/Contract approval chains, 36 custom fields across 7 core doctypes, a full Procurement Officer/Department Manager/Officer/User permission model (51 Custom DocPerm grants), 5 desk reports, 9 Client Scripts, and 2 desk pages (Procurement Dashboard, RFQ Comparison). See **Section 2 (Current State)** for the complete, live-verified inventory (generated 2026-09-09, not from memory), and Section 6 (Changelog) for the full build history — 95 versioned entries from scaffolding through the deployment audit that confirmed this app installs correctly on a genuinely fresh site.

### 1.4 Scope — Original Plan vs. What Was Actually Built

The original plan (below, written 2026-08-03) is preserved for historical record; every item on it has since been built, confirmed via Section 2's live inventory:
- Purchase Order Amendment — built (submittable custom doctype, own Workflow, own naming series)
- `Supplier ASN`, `Supplier ASN Item`, `Supplier Invoice Dispute` doctypes migrated from `supplier_portal` — built (all 3 present, module `Proc App`)
- PO acknowledgment fields and Supplier onboarding fields migrated from `supplier_portal` — built (`Purchase Order.supplier_acknowledged*`, `Supplier.portal_onboarding_status`/`commercial_registration_no`/`onboarding_*` — see Section 2.2)
- Purchase Requisition + approval workflow (OOB via Material Request + Frappe Workflow) — built (Section 4, "Material Request Approval Workflow")
- Stock Management (OOB) — confirmed working via ERPNext's own out-of-the-box functionality, no custom code needed (Section 3, Phase 3)
- Procurement and Stock reports — built (5 reports, Section 2.6)
- Roles & Permissions — built (4 project roles, full permission model, Section 2.3)

Per `PROC_SUITE_ARCHITECTURE.md` Sections 2 and 3 (OOB-vs-Custom Decision Log, Notifications Master List — relocated from `SUPPLIER_PORTAL_SPEC.md` on 2026-08-06, see Section 5 below for that record), this was the full original scope. Nothing on it remains outstanding.

| Role | Grants | Notable write/create/submit access |

| Procurement Officer | 22 | Full read/write/create/submit on Material Request, Purchase Order Amendment, Request for Quotation, RFQ Comparison Sheet, Purchase Order, Supplier Quotation, Contract, Purchase Invoice; create-only on Purchase Receipt; read-only on Supplier, Account, Contract Template, Budget, Item/Item Group/UOM/Warehouse/Brand/Company/Department/Price List, Supplier ASN |
| Department User | 9 | Read/write/create (no submit) on Material Request; read-only on Item, Item Group, UOM, Warehouse, Brand, Company, Department, Price List |
| Department Manager | 9 | Read/write (no create/submit) on Material Request; same read-only supporting-doctype set as Department User |
| Department Officer | 11 | Read/write/submit on Material Request; same supporting-doctype read-only set plus Stock Reconciliation and Stock Settings |

(`Supplier Portal User`, also checked live, belongs to `supplier_portal` — see that app's own spec.)

### 2.4 Workflows (5 active, all currently `is_active: 1`)

| Workflow | Document Type | States | Key transitions |
|---|---|---|---|
| Material Request Approval | Material Request | Draft → Pending Requesting-Dept Approval → Pending Concerned-Dept Review → (Approved - Issue \| Forwarded to Purchase \| Pending Concerned-Dept Manager Approval → Pending Procurement Approval → Approved - Purchase) \| Rejected | Submit (Department User) → Approve/Reject (Department Manager) → Approve - Issue from Stock / Approve - Forward to Purchase / Reject (Department Officer) → Approve/Reject (Department Manager) → Approve/Reject (Procurement Officer) |
| Contract Approval | Contract | Draft → Pending Approval → Approved → Terminated \| Rejected | Submit/Approve/Reject/Terminate, all Procurement Officer |
| Comparison Sheet Approval | RFQ Comparison Sheet | Draft → Pending Approval → Approved \| Rejected | Submit/Approve/Reject, all Procurement Officer |
| RFQ Approval | Request for Quotation | Draft → Pending Approval → Approved \| Rejected | Submit/Approve/Reject, all Procurement Officer |
| Purchase Order Amendment Approval | Purchase Order Amendment | Draft → Pending Approval → Approved \| Rejected | Submit/Approve/Reject, all Procurement Officer |

Purchase Order carries no Workflow at all as of 2026-09-09 — a 6th, non-project demo Workflow (`PO WF`, `is_active: 1`, generic Draft/Pending/Approved states, every transition allowed by role `"All"`) was found live during the 2026-09-08 deployment audit and removed 2026-09-09 once the one real Purchase Order it had left stranded (`PUR-ORD-2026-00034`, a genuine draft) was moved to its correct state first (Section 5).

### 2.9 Custom Icon Sprite

A single-symbol SVG sprite ships in-app at `proc_app/public/icons/proc_icons.svg` (built asset path: `/assets/proc_app/icons/proc_icons.svg`), declared via `app_include_icons` in `hooks.py` (list form, matching how `frappe`/`erpnext` both declare theirs — confirmed by reading both before writing this, not from a single example). One symbol, `icon-kcsc-procurement`, a shopping-cart glyph (handle + basket outline as one continuous stroke path, two wheel dots). **Colour is deliberately not hardcoded**: `frappe/public/scss/common/icons.scss`'s `.icon` class sets `fill`/`stroke`/`stroke-width` via CSS custom properties (`--icon-fill`/`--icon-stroke`) at the point of use, and CSS always overrides an SVG presentation attribute — confirmed live before drawing anything, not assumed. The structural path carries no colour at all; the two wheel dots use `fill="var(--icon-stroke)"` only, matching frappe's own `timeless/icons.svg` convention for a "solid dot" element (transparent fill in dark mode would otherwise render them as empty rings). `Workspace "Procurement"` (`icon`), `Workspace Sidebar "Procurement"` (`header_icon` — not `icon`, which isn't a real field on this doctype and would silently no-op if set), and the `Desktop Icon` linked to `Procurement` (`icon`) all point to `kcsc-procurement`. All three are file-based (auto-exported on save under `site1.local`'s developer_mode, independent of fixtures) AND fixture-tracked (`workspace.json`/`workspace_sidebar.json`/`desktop_icon.json`, already matched by `hooks.py`'s existing module/`link_to` filters) — both paths confirmed live to carry the new icon name.

### 2.10 Shared Portal Translation Vocabulary

`proc_app/public/js/portal_ar_shared.json` (219 keys) holds the Arabic UI vocabulary shared between `supplier_portal` and `proc_portal` — generic business/document nouns, statuses, form actions, and empty-state/error patterns that both portals genuinely use, split out of `supplier_portal`'s original 372-key file (see `SUPPLIER_PORTAL_SPEC.md` Changelog for the full shared/own split reasoning). This app owns the file because both portals already depend on `proc_app` (§1.2, `PROC_SUITE_ARCHITECTURE.md` §1) while neither portal depends on the other — placing shared vocabulary here adds zero new edges to that dependency graph. Consumed by each portal's own `api/lang.py` (`_get_shared_translations()`, loaded via `frappe.get_app_path("proc_app", ...)`, never a hardcoded path) and merged with that portal's own, smaller `portal_ar.json` (own keys win on conflict). This file is plain static JSON — not fixture-tracked, no doctype involved, ships and updates like any other `public/` asset via normal git history.

### 2.11 Desk Arabic Translation Overrides

Separate mechanism from §2.10 above — that file covers the two custom *portals'* own UI copy; this one covers erpnext/frappe's own **desk** field labels and Select options (doctype metadata, not portal templates), where a small number of the bundled `ar.csv` translations were found to be wrong via a dedicated audit.

**Why a `Translation` doctype record, not an edit to erpnext's `ar.csv`:** `ar.csv` is owned and shipped by erpnext itself — any direct edit would be silently overwritten by the next `bench update`/erpnext upgrade, with no warning. Frappe's own translation resolution (confirmed by reading `frappe/translate.py`'s `get_all_translations()` → `_merge_translations()`, then proven empirically with a throwaway record) builds the merged dictionary from every installed app's `ar.csv`/`.mo` files first, then calls `.update(get_user_translations(lang))` — a plain dict `update()` where `Translation` doctype records (`get_user_translations()`, reading `frappe.get_all("Translation", filters={"language": lang})`) are applied *last* and win on an exact `source_text` (+ optional `context`) match. This is the standard, UI-maintainable override path Frappe itself provides for exactly this situation, survives upgrades by construction, and needs no core patch.

**The six corrections** (found via a desk-wide audit across the 25 doctypes a procurement user actually touches — 810 distinct field labels/Select options/doctype names, 698 already correctly translated, 112 with no Arabic at all, and a further collision scan for cases where two unrelated English terms had been mapped to the same Arabic word):

| Source (English) | Old (wrong) | New | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| `Amount` | `كمية` ("quantity") | `المبلغ` | Collided with `Quantity`, which also resolves to `كمية` — a monetary value and a count are different concepts; `Quantity` itself is untouched and still correctly returns `كمية`. |
| `Draft` | `مشروع` ("project") | `مسودة` | Collided with `Project`, which also resolves to `مشروع`. `مسودة` is the correct word for a draft/unsaved document state, and is already what proc_portal's own glossary uses for the same concept. |
| `Signee` | `الموقع` ("website"/"the location") | `المُوقِّع` | Collided with `Website`, which also resolves to `الموقع` — a real field on `Contract` (`contract.signee`), a person who signs, confused with a URL. `Website` itself is untouched and still correctly returns `الموقع`. |
| `Paid From Account Type` | `نوع الحساب المدفوع` (direction lost) | `نوع الحساب المدفوع منه` | Collided with `Paid To Account Type` — opposite accounting concepts (source vs. destination account) were mapped to the identical Arabic phrase. |
| `Paid To Account Type` | `نوع الحساب المدفوع` (direction lost) | `نوع الحساب المدفوع إليه` | Same collision as above, the other half of the pair. |
| `Party User` | `مستخدم الحزب` ("user of the political party") | `مستخدم الطرف` | Not a collision with another string, but a standalone mistranslation on `Contract` — `حزب` means political party/faction; `الطرف` (party as in "the other party to a contract") is what `Party Type`/`Party Name` on the very same doctype already correctly use. |

**Verified before shipping**: re-running the audit's own collision scan after creating the six records shows all four genuine semantic collisions resolved (the pairs above no longer share an Arabic value) and confirms no new collision was introduced — the pre-existing 12 harmless collisions (near-synonyms/spelling variants like "Tax ID"/"Tax Id", "Notes"/"Remarks", genuinely fine to share a word) are untouched, exactly as before.

**Deployment**: the six `Translation` records are fixture-tracked in `hooks.py`, filtered by `language="ar"` + `source_text in [the six strings]` + `contributed=0` — `source_text` rather than the records' own autoname keeps the filter self-documenting (a reader of `hooks.py` sees exactly which six strings are covered without decoding an opaque name), and `Translation`'s own community-contribution feature (`contributed`/`contribution_status` fields, otherwise unrelated to this fixture) is explicitly excluded so a future contributed translation for one of these same six English strings can never be silently swept into this fixture and shipped as if it were a reviewed correction. Verified live on a second site (`deploytest.local`, migrated from a zero-`Translation`-record baseline): all six records arrive via `bench migrate` and take effect identically.

**Extended to 98 total (2026-09-11):** the same audit's 112 previously-untranslated strings were classified (hidden fields, technical naming-series patterns, genuinely user-facing, or already-covered by the portal glossary — `desk_untranslated.txt`) and the 92 that needed real translation work were reviewed and added the same way, bringing the fixture to 98 `Translation` records total. The `hooks.py` filter's `source_text in [...]` list was extended to match (all 98 named explicitly), not switched to a broader `language="ar" + contributed=0` filter with no name list — `contributed=0` is the *default* for any manually-created Translation record, including one a future admin adds by hand through the normal desk UI for an unrelated, unreviewed reason; a broad filter would ship that too, indistinguishably from these reviewed corrections. This keeps the same explicit-list convention this file already uses for Workflow States/Actions/Reports/Roles, just larger.

**Regression check before shipping the 92** (several of these source strings are common desk vocabulary shared with unrelated ERPNext doctypes, not scoped to our 25 — `Approved`, `Shipped`, `References`, `Advances`, `Terms`, `Alias`, `Requested By`): checked every one live, across both core `DocField` and `Custom Field` records site-wide, not assumed safe from the string alone. `Approved`/`Shipped`/`Requested By` turned out to exist only on our own custom fields (`Supplier.portal_onboarding_status`, `Supplier ASN.status`, `Purchase Order Amendment.requested_by`) — zero foreign exposure. `References`/`Advances`/`Terms` are genuinely used on several unrelated core doctypes (`Sales Invoice`, `POS Invoice`, `Quotation`, `Delivery Note`, etc.) as the same generic section-label concept (linked reference documents, advance-payment allocation, terms-and-conditions) — the same Arabic word (`المراجع`/`الدفعات المقدَّمة`/`الشروط`) reads correctly in all of them, not a collision. Re-ran the audit's own collision scan afterward: identical to the six-correction baseline, zero new collisions introduced by any of the 92. No string was found too generic to translate safely site-wide, so no context-scoped record was needed for any of them. Verified live on `deploytest.local` again: all 98 records (not just the original six) arrive via `bench migrate` and resolve correctly, including that `Quantity`/`Website` remain untouched.

**A repeat of the same accidental-fixture-sweep risk, caught both times**: `bench export-fixtures --app proc_app` re-exports *every* fixture listed in `hooks.py`, not just the one being worked on — both times this task's own `export-fixtures` run picked up `workflow.json`'s `is_active` reflecting this dev site's own runtime-activated workflows (a deliberate, permanent `is_active: 0` default documented in Section 5's Known Issues, not something either export should ever change), and the second run also picked up incidental `migration_hash`/`modified` bookkeeping noise on three unrelated doctypes. Both reverted before committing either time; the standing lesson (now written down explicitly rather than re-learned a third time) is to review the *entire* diff after any `export-fixtures` run in this app, not just the fixture file actually being changed.

**Extended to 432 total (2026-09-11) — semantic review, not just missing/colliding translations:** the first two rounds above only caught strings with no Arabic at all or two English terms colliding on one Arabic word; neither could catch a translation that's grammatically valid but means the wrong thing (`ID` → `هوية شخصية`, "personal ID card", for a plain document/record identifier; `Required By` → `المطلوبة من قبل`, "required by [a person]", for a deadline date; `Filtered` → `منقى`, "purified", for a list-view filter). A full extraction of all 816 already-Arabic desk strings (`desk_review.txt`) was reviewed by hand, in five batches, producing 334 corrections — the largest round yet, bringing the fixture total to 432.

Verified before applying: all five batch files round-tripped with zero encoding failures (uploaded as real files, not pasted — the one time a translation batch WAS pasted inline earlier in this project, it silently lost bytes in the 0x80–0x9F range; every subsequent batch has been a real file upload since). Checked for conflicts both across the five batches against each other (334 distinct `source_text` values, zero appearing in more than one batch) and against the 98 records already shipped (zero overlap) before creating anything.

**Regression check, same discipline as the 92 before them, wider net this time** (`Quantity`, `Warehouse`, `Item`, `Rate`, `Date`, `Description`, `Company`, `Project`, `Reference`, `Received`, `Completed`, `Open`, `Other`, `Total` — some of the most generic vocabulary in the entire desk, not scoped to procurement at all): checked all 14 across both `DocField` and `Custom Field` site-wide. All read correctly in every foreign context found (Sales/Stock/Manufacturing/Support doctypes all use the exact same concept for each of these — there is essentially no more-generic vocabulary in ERPNext than "Date"/"Description"/"Company", and the review holds up at that scale).

Re-running the collision scan surfaced two that need real attention, not filed as harmless like the rest:

| Arabic | English pair | Where it actually collides | Assessment |
|---|---|---|---|
| `العنوان` | `Address` / `Title` | `Purchase Invoice`, `Purchase Receipt`, `Supplier Quotation` — three of our own 25 doctypes carry **both** fields, both labeled `العنوان` | Genuine ambiguity: a user on one of these forms sees two different fields — a physical/dispatch address and the document's own title — with an identical label. `العنوان` is a legitimate, common Arabic rendering of *both* senses (this isn't a mistranslation of either word individually), but the co-occurrence is real and worth a follow-up decision, not silently accepted. Reported, not fixed — a context-scoped `Translation` record (using the `context` field this doctype already supports) is the likely fix if this gets prioritized. |
| `جهة الاتصال` | `Contact` / `Contact Person` | `Purchase Invoice`, `Purchase Receipt`, `Supplier Quotation` (same three) plus several unrelated doctypes | Milder — `Contact` is typically a link to a Contact record and `Contact Person` its display name, close enough in practice that `جهة الاتصال` ("the point of contact") reads sensibly for both, but flagged for the same reason: real co-occurrence on our own forms, not just a theoretical pairing. |

The remaining 21 full-scan collision groups (23 total minus these 2) are the same class already established as harmless in the two prior rounds — near-synonyms, abbreviation pairs, or case/whitespace variants of one term (`Billed Amount`/`Billed Amt`, `Qty as Per Stock UOM`/`Qty as per Stock UOM`, `In Transit`/`Transit`, etc.) — including `Write Off`/`Writeoff`, corrected in this batch from the erpnext-default `لا تصلح` ("not valid") to `شطب`, the standard accounting term, with both spellings now sharing the corrected value rather than the wrong one. No string among the 334 was judged too generic to safely override site-wide — the two flagged above are collisions between two *specific* strings on shared forms, not a single string that's unsafe on its own.

`hooks.py`'s `source_text in [...]` list extended from 98 to all 432 names, same explicit-list convention, same reasoning against a broader `contributed=0`-only filter. Verified live on `deploytest.local` (migrated from the 98-record baseline): all 432 arrive and resolve correctly, including that `Amount`/`Draft`/`Quantity`/`Website` from the earlier rounds remain correct and untouched. The same accidental-fixture-sweep risk recurred a third time (`doctype.json`'s `migration_hash`/`modified` bookkeeping noise on the same three doctypes) and was reverted before committing, same as before; `workflow.json` did not recur this round since no runtime re-activation had happened since the last correction.

**The `Address`/`Title` and `Contact`/`Contact Person` collisions, fixed via context-scoped records (2026-09-12):** these two pairs couldn't be resolved by picking one better global word each, the way the 334 above were — `Purchase Invoice`, `Purchase Receipt`, and `Supplier Quotation` (all three of our 25 doctypes) each carry all four fields on the same form (`address_display`/`contact_display`, read-only computed text; `title`/`contact_person`, the underlying Data/Link fields), so a global rename of any one of them would just move the collision to the other member of its pair everywhere else in the system where that word is used correctly on its own.

Investigated whether Frappe's `Translation.context` field is a real, load-bearing mechanism for field labels before proposing it as the fix, rather than assuming: read `frappe/model/document.py`/`base_document.py`, which use `_(df.label, context=df.parent)` for validation messages — `df.parent` is the owning doctype name — establishing `context = doctype name` as the existing, standard convention, not something invented for this fix. Confirmed the same holds for the actual desk *form UI* (not just error messages): `frappe/public/js/frappe/form/controls/base_input.js`'s `set_label()` — the function that sets every ordinary field's visible `<label>` text — calls `__(this.df.label, null, this.df.parent)`, and `frappe/public/js/frappe/translate.js`'s `frappe._` mirrors the exact same `"msg:context"` lookup key Python uses, reading from `frappe._messages`, which is populated at boot directly from `get_all_translations()` (the same merged dict `Translation` records feed into) — so a context-scoped record genuinely reaches the browser and the exact function that renders desk field labels, not a mechanism that exists in the doctype but is bypassed by the actual rendering path.

Proved it empirically before shipping anything, both at the Python level (`_("Address", context="Purchase Invoice")` picks up a throwaway override; the same call with no context, or with an unrelated doctype's context, correctly falls through to the global value — created and removed before touching anything real) and on an actual rendered desk form: `address_display`/`contact_display` are hidden by Frappe's own client-side "don't show an empty computed field" behavior when unset, and no Purchase Invoice/Receipt/Supplier Quotation on this site had real address/contact data populated to make them appear — so the two fields were given temporary placeholder values on one existing test document, the "Address & Contact" tab was opened in a real headless-Chromium session (this sandbox has neither a system browser nor passwordless `sudo`; the same `dpkg-deb`-extracted-library technique from the RTL work was reused), the three labels were read as genuinely distinct text on the same visible tab (`عنوان المورد` / `العنوان` / `معلومات الاتصال`), and the placeholder values were reverted immediately after.

Chose `Address` → `عنوان المورد` ("Supplier's Address") and `Contact` → `معلومات الاتصال` ("Contact Information") as the two doctype-scoped overrides, leaving `Title` (`العنوان`) and `Contact Person` (`جهة الاتصال`) on their existing, already-correct global translations — the minimal fix that removes the ambiguity (6 new records: 2 strings × 3 doctypes) rather than touching all four. Re-ran the collision scan afterward: unchanged at the global (context-less) level, exactly as expected, since the fix is deliberately scoped and doesn't touch the global values at all — the meaningful check is context-aware, and confirms both pairs now resolve distinctly on all three doctypes while an unrelated doctype's context (or no context) still correctly falls through to the shared global word.

**Fixture mechanics, not just a bigger list this time:** a context-scoped record shares its `source_text` with an existing global entry, so it can't be told apart by `source_text` alone — and a *second* `{"dt": "Translation", ...}` fixture entry was deliberately not used either, since `export_fixtures()` names its output file purely from the doctype (confirmed by reading `frappe/utils/fixtures.py`, not assumed), so a second entry for the same doctype would silently overwrite this one's `translation.json` on the next export rather than adding to it. Extended the single existing entry instead: `filters` (the plain `language`/`contributed` AND-conditions) stays, and a new `or_filters` ORs the existing 432-string `source_text` list with a match on `context` being one of the three affected doctypes — confirmed live via `frappe.get_all()` with the identical filter shape that it matches exactly 438 (432 + 6) before writing it into `hooks.py`. Verified live on `deploytest.local` (migrated from the 432-record baseline): all 438 arrive and resolve correctly, both pairs distinct on all three doctypes, global values still untouched. The same `doctype.json` bookkeeping noise recurred a fourth time on `export-fixtures` and was reverted before committing, as every time before.

## 4. Material Request Approval Workflow (Built and Live)

### 4.1 Purpose

This documents the design for proc_app's first real feature: a Material Request approval workflow, built entirely on ERPNext v16 OOB capabilities (Material Request doctype + Frappe Workflow engine) plus 4 custom fields. Designed 2026-08-07, based on a real example workflow provided by KCSC, before the client workshop — treated as a standard baseline per the same "adopt now, refine later" principle as Section 1.6 of PROC_SUITE_ARCHITECTURE.md.

### 4.2 Reference Example (source of this design)

A Finance Department employee needs printer ink. They create a Material Request addressed to IT (the department that manages that item type). The Finance Department Manager approves first. Then IT's "person in charge" (Officer) checks stock: if available, it's issued directly from IT's warehouse to Finance; if not, it goes to IT's Manager for approval, then to Procurement's Officer for final approval, before becoming a Purchase Order.

### 4.3 Custom Fields Required

On Material Request:
- requesting_department (Link to Department) — the department that needs the item, e.g. Finance
- concerned_department (Link to Department) — the department that manages/reviews that item type, e.g. IT

On Department:
- department_manager (Link to User) — this department's manager, approves requests where this department is either the requesting or concerned department
- department_officer (Link to User) — this department's person in charge, reviews stock availability and decides the issue-vs-purchase branch when this department is the concerned department

### 4.4 New Roles Required

- Department Manager — generic role, held by whoever manages any department. Actual approval authority for a specific request is enforced via a Workflow transition condition checking that the acting user matches the relevant department's department_manager field, not by the role alone.
- Department Officer — same pattern, checked against department_officer.
- Procurement's final approval reuses the existing draft Procurement Officer role (see PROC_SUITE_ARCHITECTURE.md, Section 1.6) — no new role needed there.

### 4.5 Workflow States & Transitions

Draft, then Submit moves it to Pending Requesting-Department Approval, where the approver is the requesting department's department_manager. Approve moves it to Pending Concerned-Department Review, where the approver is the concerned department's department_officer. From there, two branches: Approve as Issue from Stock moves it directly to Approved – Issue (docstatus: Submitted). Approve as Forward to Purchase moves it to Pending Concerned-Department Manager Approval, where the approver is the concerned department's department_manager; Approve there moves it to Pending Procurement Approval, where the approver holds the Procurement Officer role; Approve there moves it to Approved – Purchase (docstatus: Submitted). A Reject action is available at every approval step and moves the request to Rejected.

Each department-scoped approval uses a Workflow Transition condition (confirmed available on Frappe's Workflow Transition doctype, condition field, Code type) — e.g., a transition is only enabled for the acting user if they match the relevant department's department_manager or department_officer field for the specific department linked on that request. This means the same 2 roles work correctly for every department, without per-department configuration beyond setting the 2 Department fields.

Approved – Issue and Approved – Purchase are both docstatus: Submitted states — at that point, ERPNext's existing native Create actions (confirmed present, no custom code needed) become available: make_stock_entry() for the issue path, make_purchase_order() / make_request_for_quotation() for the purchase path.

### 4.6 Deliberately Deferred (Not Built in This Phase)

- Free-text / not-yet-in-system items: ERPNext's Material Request Item.item_code is mandatory at the Frappe framework level (confirmed via code audit, not just a UI restriction) — allowing a supplier or requester to describe an item that doesn't exist yet in the Item master would require genuine custom code (a flag + description field, plus a script to auto-create the Item record at PO time). This is a real, scoped piece of custom work, deliberately deferred until the core approval workflow is built and proven. Not forgotten — tracked here.
- Approval value thresholds: still pending the client workshop (see PROC_SUITE_ARCHITECTURE.md, Section 1.6) — this workflow's approvals are role/department-based, not value-based, for now.
- **Self-approval:** `allow_self_approval` was left at Frappe's default (`1`, allowed) on all Workflow transitions. This was a deliberate decision (2026-08-08), not an oversight — confirmed explicitly rather than assumed. If a single person holds both the requester's identity and the relevant approval role for a given request, they are permitted to approve their own request at any stage.
- **Submit-step department scoping:** The initial Draft → Submit transition only checks that the acting user holds the "Department User" role — it does not verify they actually belong to the specific `requesting_department` they're filing the request on behalf of. Enforcing that would require an Employee-to-Department assignment concept that doesn't exist yet in this design. Deliberately deferred, consistent with "start standard, refine later" — anyone with the Department User role can currently file a request on behalf of any department.
- **Permission hook timing differs from supplier_portal's pattern:** `proc_app`'s DocPerm setup (`setup.py`) is registered via `after_migrate`, not `after_install` like `supplier_portal`'s equivalent. This was a deliberate choice (2026-08-08): `after_install` only fires once, at initial app installation, which made it untestable on an already-installed app during development. `after_migrate` re-asserts permissions on every migrate, which is arguably more robust long-term (self-healing if permissions ever drift) at the cost of running slightly more often. Idempotency confirmed via delete-then-recreate before each grant — safe to run repeatedly.
- **Workflow activation is not fixture-managed:** `is_active` on the "Material Request Approval" Workflow document stays committed as `0` in `fixtures/workflow.json` permanently — this is the correct, safe default for a fresh client install (a workflow should never auto-activate before an admin has configured departments, managers, and officers). Discovered 2026-08-08: `bench migrate` re-syncs the Workflow from its fixture on every run, silently reverting a runtime activation back to `0`. **Correct activation procedure** (also discovered 2026-08-08, the hard way): do NOT toggle `is_active` via `frappe.db.set_value()` — this updates the database but does not invalidate Frappe's `workflow` cache key (`frappe.cache.hget("workflow", <doctype>)`), which is checked with a `is None` test, not a falsy check — so a stale cached empty string persists indefinitely even after the DB is corrected, silently disabling workflow_state defaulting and all transition validation. The correct way to activate/deactivate is via the standard Document API (`doc = frappe.get_doc("Workflow", ...); doc.is_active = 1; doc.save()`), which triggers normal cache invalidation, OR by explicitly clearing the cache key afterward (`frappe.cache.hdel("workflow", "Material Request")`) if a direct DB update is used. **Operational consequence:** activating the workflow for real use (or for testing) must be done as a deliberate action after every `migrate`, using `doc.save()` — not raw SQL/ORM value-setting — and must not persist to the fixture.
- **Supporting doctype read permissions found incomplete via manual browser testing:** console-based testing (`doc.insert()` under a test user) only exercises document-level and field-level permission checks — it does not exercise the desk UI's Link field dropdowns, which independently check read permission on the linked doctype when a user opens a Link field to search/select a value. Yasser's manual browser test surfaced two more doctypes needing read access that console testing had missed: `Company` and `Department`. Added to `SUPPORTING_DOCTYPES` in `setup.py` (2026-08-09), bringing the read-only supporting-doctype list to `Item`, `Item Group`, `UOM`, `Warehouse`, `Brand`, `Company`, `Department` — all 4 workflow roles, read-only. Applied directly via `setup_supporting_doctype_permissions()` (not a full `migrate`, to avoid re-triggering the Workflow `is_active` revert documented above). Worth remembering for future permission gaps: UI-driven testing and console-driven testing surface different failure classes — neither alone is sufficient.
- **Department Officer requires ERPNext's native "Stock User" role for the Issue-from-Stock branch:** Discovered via manual browser testing (2026-08-09) — creating a Stock Entry from a Material Request in "Approved - Issue" state requires permissions on the Stock Entry doctype itself, which our custom permission matrix (Section 3, setup.py) never granted (we only gave read-only access to Warehouse as a supporting doctype, not write/create/submit on Stock Entry). Rather than expand Department Officer's own Custom DocPerm to cover this, the correct fix is assigning ERPNext's existing native "Stock User" role (a standard OOB role, not something proc_app manages) to whichever user account is acting as Department Officer for a given department — keeping Department Officer itself narrowly scoped to department-approval concerns only, and stock-transaction rights separate. **Operational consequence:** any real person assigned as a department's `department_officer` must also hold ERPNext's standard "Stock User" role (or equivalent) to actually execute the Issue-from-Stock action — this is a per-deployment user-assignment step, not something proc_app's fixtures configure automatically, consistent with the standing "roles in code, user assignment in the desk" principle.

### 4.7 Live Test Results (2026-08-08) — PASSED

The full purchase-branch path was tested end-to-end against real Frappe permission/workflow enforcement (using `apply_workflow()`, not manual field manipulation), with 5 test users and 2 real seeded departments (Accounts - K as requesting, Operations - K as concerned).

**Confirmed working:**
- Draft → Submit (by Department User)
- Requesting-Dept Approval, correctly scoped to the specific department's manager — a wrong-department Department Manager was tested and correctly blocked (`WorkflowTransitionError`), document state unaffected
- Concerned-Dept Review — a wrong-role attempt (Department Manager instead of Department Officer) was tested and correctly blocked at the role gate itself, before any condition was evaluated
- Approve - Forward to Purchase branch, correctly routed to Concerned-Dept Manager Approval
- Concerned-Dept Manager Approval → Procurement Approval → **Approved - Purchase**, with `docstatus` correctly transitioning 0 → 1 (genuinely submitted, confirmed via direct DB query, not just workflow_state label)

**Not yet tested:** the Issue-from-Stock branch (Approve - Issue from Stock → Approved - Issue), and the Reject path at each stage. Both use the same proven mechanisms (role gate + department condition) as the tested purchase branch, so are expected to work, but should be verified explicitly before considering the workflow fully proven in all paths.

**Environment state after testing:** test document deleted (cancelled then force-deleted) to leave a clean slate. Workflow left `is_active: 1` at the database level per request — note this is NOT reflected in the committed fixture (still `0`, correctly, per Section 3.6) and will silently revert to inactive on the next `bench migrate`, consistent with the documented activation procedure.

### 4.8 Redesign — Forward to Purchase Now Spawns a Linked Document (2026-08-09)

**Problem found via manual browser testing:** ERPNext's native "Create" button on Material Request is gated entirely by `material_request_type`, not `workflow_state`. The original design (one document, type "Purchase" throughout, branching internally between issue/purchase) meant the Issue-from-Stock branch never had a working native path to create a Stock Entry — ERPNext only wires `make_stock_entry()` to the Material Transfer, Material Issue, and Customer Provided types, never Purchase.

**Redesign:** Requests are now created as `material_request_type = "Material Issue"` from the start.
- **Issue-from-Stock branch:** unchanged in structure — same document reaches `Approved - Issue`. Since the type is now Material Issue, ERPNext's native "Issue Material" Create button works correctly with zero custom UI needed.
- **Forward-to-Purchase branch:** the original document now transitions to a new terminal state, `Forwarded to Purchase` (docstatus: 1), and is also stopped via ERPNext's native `update_status("Stopped")` mechanism (confirmed reliable via code audit — correctly survives `status_map` recomputation, not a raw field write). A new custom field, `source_material_request` (Link → Material Request, read-only), and a new hook (`proc_app/material_request_hooks.py`, registered via `doc_events.Material Request.on_update`) automatically spawns a **new, separate** Material Request with `material_request_type = "Purchase"`, copying items, linking back via `source_material_request`, and pre-positioned directly at `Pending Concerned-Dept Manager Approval` — skipping the Requesting-Dept Approval and Concerned-Dept Review steps that already happened on the original. This new document then completes its own approval chain (Concerned-Dept Manager → Procurement) to reach `Approved - Purchase`, at which point ERPNext's native Purchase Order/RFQ Create buttons work correctly, since its type is genuinely "Purchase."

**Why this approach over a custom button:** avoids fighting ERPNext's native gating logic entirely — both resulting documents have a `material_request_type` that honestly matches their actual native behavior, so no custom UI/buttons are needed anywhere in the flow.

**Hook idempotency:** guarded against re-triggering on every subsequent save (checks `get_doc_before_save()`) and against duplicate spawning (checks for an existing linked document before creating another).

**Tested 2026-08-09, end-to-end, zero exceptions:** both branches proven — Issue-from-Stock (manual browser test) and Forward-to-Purchase spawn-and-complete (console test, full chain from Draft through both documents reaching their respective terminal states).

## 5. Known Issues & Decisions Log

| Date | Type | Description | Decision / Resolution |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2026-09-09 | Found & Fixed (real cache regression during removal) | Removing the "PO WF" demo Workflow (below) — confirmed safe first: the one real PO it had left in a workflow-specific state, `PUR-ORD-2026-00034` (`docstatus: 0`, genuinely a draft, `status: Draft`), was moved to `workflow_state: "Draft"` via `db_set()` before the Workflow was deleted, so nothing was stranded mid-operation. **Immediately after deleting the Workflow, creating a brand-new Purchase Order started failing outright** with `DoesNotExistError: Workflow PO WF not found` — surfaced live, not assumed safe just because the delete itself succeeded. Traced to a stale cache: `frappe.model.document.Document.validate_workflow()` (called on every `insert()`) looks up which Workflow applies to the doctype, and that lookup is cached — the cache still held "PO WF" as the answer for Purchase Order from before the deletion. `bench clear-cache` resolved it completely: a fresh PO insert succeeded with `workflow_state: None` (correctly unset, no workflow drives it anymore), and a real call to `proc_portal.api.purchase_orders.submit_po()` on that same document also succeeded end to end. **Standing rule for this project, now explicit**: deleting an active Workflow must always be followed by `bench clear-cache` before considering the operation complete — the delete itself is not sufficient, confirmed live. **Second, distinct lesson found 2026-09-09, a few hours later**: a genuinely unrelated symptom surfaced next — "Workflow not found" errors appearing broadly across the desk. Root-caused, NOT a caching issue this time (confirmed `clear-cache`/`clear-website-cache`/rebuild does NOT fix it — reproduced the exact failing call, `get_transitions()` on `PUR-ORD-2026-00034`, still throwing afterward): deleting a Workflow document does **not** clean up already-created `Workflow Action` records tied to it — 4 orphaned rows were found (1 still `status: Open` for `PUR-ORD-2026-00034`; 3 already `Completed`, across `PUR-ORD-2026-00034` and `PUR-ORD-2026-00029`). The visible error came from a third-party app already installed on this site, `kashef_chat` (unrelated to this project), whose desk-wide launcher badge count polls every open `Workflow Action` row and calls `frappe.model.workflow.get_transitions(doc)` on each — and core Frappe's own `get_workflow()` has no guard for "this doctype has zero active workflows," unconditionally trying to load a Workflow named `""` and throwing `DoesNotExistError: Workflow  not found` the moment such a row exists for a doctype whose workflow was since deleted. **Fixed** by deleting all 4 orphaned `Workflow Action` rows directly (`frappe.delete_doc(..., force=True)`, child `Workflow Action Permitted Role` rows went with them automatically); re-verified via the real third-party endpoint itself (`kashef_chat.api.chat.get_launcher_badge_count()`, not just a console simulation) succeeding cleanly afterward. **Note, precisely stated rather than glossed over**: `get_transitions()` called directly against `PUR-ORD-2026-00034` still throws today, and correctly so — that doctype genuinely has no active workflow, and this core function was never written to be called against a document with none; the actual fix is that no code path in the real request flow reaches that unsafe call anymore, since the tracking rows that caused it to be reached are gone. **Standing rule, extended**: deleting an active Workflow must be followed by both `bench clear-cache` (for the insert-time lookup cache) AND a check for orphaned `Workflow Action` rows referencing that doctype (for anything, ours or third-party, that iterates them) — the two are independent failure modes with independent fixes, not the same bug twice. Documented as procedure, not patched in core: this is Frappe's own missing guard plus a third-party app's polling pattern, not something to work around in `proc_app`'s own code. | Cache cleared; re-verified insert + `submit_po()` both work cleanly post-clear. Same standing lesson as the Contract Workflow conflict (`PROC_APP_SPEC.md` v1.92) reinforced here in a new, more severe way: an ungated demo Workflow (every transition allowed by role `"All"`) is a real security gap on a live site, not merely clutter — removing it is correct, but the removal itself has a real operational step (cache invalidation) that isn't optional. All 4 orphaned `Workflow Action` rows deleted (2026-09-09); confirmed zero remain for either affected PO, and the real `kashef_chat` badge-count endpoint now succeeds. |
| 2026-09-08 | Found & Fixed (real deployment test, severe) | A genuine install of all three apps on a fresh site (`deploytest.local`, not reasoning about fixtures) found `proc_app`'s own `install-app` **fails outright** with `Not in Developer Mode` — and the same failure blocks `bench migrate` too, meaning `after_migrate`'s entire Custom DocPerm permission setup (51 grants across 4 roles) never runs on a fresh site at all, install or migrate. Root cause, traced precisely via the full traceback: `frappe/core/doctype/page/page.py`'s `Page.validate()` unconditionally throws for a new Page record outside developer mode, with **no exemption for `frappe.flags.in_import`/`in_migrate`/`in_patch`** — unlike every other fixture-tracked doctype here (confirmed via source audit: `Report.validate_standard_report()` explicitly exempts `in_import`; `Workspace`/`Workspace Sidebar`/`Desktop Icon` only gate an *optional* file-export side effect, never throw; `DocType.check_developer_mode()` is bypassed by `custom=1`, true for all 3 of our DB-only doctypes). Because `frappe.utils.fixtures.import_fixtures()` only catches `ImportError`/`DoesNotExistError` per file (confirmed via its own source — no supported way to make one failing fixture file non-fatal to the rest), this one `ValidationError` silently aborted every fixture file sorting alphabetically after `page.json`: `property_setter`, `report`, `role`, `workflow`, `workflow_action_master`, `workflow_state`, `workspace`, `workspace_sidebar` — on a fresh install AND on any subsequent migrate. | **Fixed** (Changelog v1.95): removed the `Page` fixture entry from `hooks.py` entirely, and deleted the now-orphaned `fixtures/page.json` file itself — confirmed the file, not just the `hooks.py` config entry, must be removed, since `import_fixtures()` blindly imports every `.json` physically present in the `fixtures/` folder regardless of what `hooks.py` currently declares (found live: removing only the config entry left the stale file still breaking every subsequent install). Both Pages this covered (`rfq-comparison`, `procurement-dash`) are already file-based and sync automatically via `frappe.model.sync.sync_all()`, independent of fixtures entirely — this entry was pure, actively-dangerous redundancy. **Re-verified against a genuine reinstall + migrate on `deploytest.local`**: both now succeed with exit code 0, and the full inventory afterward matches `site1.local`'s baseline exactly across every doctype checked (Role, Workflow, Workflow State/Action Master, Property Setter incl. `Contract-main-field_order`, Custom DocPerm — 51/51, all matching). **Standing lesson, generalised beyond this one incident**: before adding any new fixture-tracked doctype to `hooks.py`, check that doctype's own core `validate()`/`on_update()` for an unconditional `developer_mode` guard with no `in_import` exemption — this is now a real, confirmed failure mode for this project, not a theoretical one. |
| 2026-09-08 | Found & Fixed (deployment gap, full audit) | A deployment audit (installing on a fresh site) found `Property Setter` was never fixture-tracked by ANY of this project's three apps, despite carrying genuinely load-bearing project content — most consequentially `Contract-main-field_order` (the doctype's *entire* field layout, separate from each Custom Field's own `insert_after`) and the naming-series options for both database-only custom doctypes (`RFQ Comparison Sheet`, `Purchase Order Amendment` — confirmed via source that neither has a file-based doctype JSON at all, so their naming series came ENTIRELY from these untracked Property Setters). None of this would have transferred to a fresh install. | Added a new `Property Setter` fixture using `or_filters` (confirmed supported by `frappe.utils.fixtures.export_fixtures`, same mechanism as `frappe.get_all`'s own `or_filters`): `doc_type IN [Contract, Purchase Order Amendment, RFQ Comparison Sheet, RFQ Comparison Sheet Item, Contract Fulfilment Checklist]` (doctypes this project fully owns — confirmed live that ZERO of the site's other ~154 Property Setters carry any of these `doc_type`s, so nothing foreign is swept in) OR `name IN [the 3 default_print_format setters on Purchase Order/Purchase Invoice/RFQ]` (named explicitly since those 3 doctypes are shared, heavily ERPNext-customised doctypes where a broader `doc_type` filter would also capture a large amount of generic, non-project noise — `accounting_dimensions_section` visibility, `scan_barcode`, `rounded_total`, etc. — confirmed live via a direct owner/module comparison that neither field reliably distinguishes project-relevant Property Setters from the site's ~154 standard ones: both sets show `owner=Administrator, module=None` uniformly). 16 records captured, verified complete against everything live and project-relevant. |
| 2026-09-08 | Found & Fixed (structural, name-list fragility) | The same audit found 9 of proc_app's 11 fixture filters used explicit name-IN lists — anything created later without also updating the list is silently excluded on a fresh install, the single largest ongoing deployment risk in this project. | Investigated each filter individually rather than blanket-converting: **DocType** and **Client Script** switched to `module`/`dt`-based filters (confirmed live to capture exactly the same record sets as the old lists, nothing foreign — DocType additionally excludes the 3 file-based doctypes it would otherwise also catch, since `sync_fixtures()` runs AFTER `sync_all()`'s own file-based doctype sync in `frappe/migrate.py`, so fixture-tracking an already-file-synced doctype risks a stale fixture snapshot silently overwriting a newer file-based edit on a later migrate). **Page/Workspace/Workspace Sidebar** switched to `module="Proc App"` (same record sets, self-maintaining for future additions). **Custom Field** switched to `dt IN [our 7 doctypes]`, catching 2 genuinely-missing fields (`Contract-custom_remarks`, `Contract-custom_cost_center`) that had never been added to the old name list — but this filter alone would ALSO have swept in `Purchase Order-workflow_state`, a field auto-created by the unrelated "PO WF" demo Workflow (see the entry below) — excluded explicitly via `name NOT IN [...]`, confirmed not ours by its own creation date/owner. **Left as deliberate name lists, confirmed not viable to change**: `Role` and `Workflow State`/`Workflow Action Master` have no `module`/`document_type` field at all (confirmed via `get_meta().has_field()`, not assumed); `Report` because only 2 of our 5 real reports carry `module="Proc App"` — the other 3 (raw-SQL, non-script reports) are filed under `Buying`/`Stock` by Frappe's own report-module inference, so a module filter would silently drop 3 of 5 real reports; **`Workflow` most deliberately of all** — a `document_type`-based filter would auto-sweep any future demo/test Workflow on a doctype we own straight into the fixture, exactly the failure mode two separate incidents this project has already hit (see below and the Contract Workflow conflict entry) — the explicit list forces a conscious decision to add each real workflow by name. |

Confirmed live it does NOT block `proc_portal.api.purchase_orders.submit_po()` (which calls `doc.submit()` directly) — a disposable test PO submitted successfully, bypassing the workflow's own Review/Approve chain entirely, with Frappe just passively syncing `workflow_state` to match the resulting `docstatus`. Deleting "PO WF" now would strand that document's `workflow_state` with no workflow left to interpret it. | **Deliberately not deleted**, per this task's own explicit stop condition (a real document would be stranded) — the same condition that correctly did NOT block the Contract Workflow removal (where every real contract's state was shared by both workflows). `Purchase Order-workflow_state` (the Custom Field Frappe auto-created alongside "PO WF") excluded from the Custom Field fixture in the meantime, so it doesn't ship regardless of what happens to the workflow itself.
